Model accuracy.

The model-accuracy lemma used for the homogeneous RB setting is a special
case of the corresponding lemmas for the heterogeneous WCMDP setting. We state it below.

Lemma 7.1

Fix
\eta\in(0,1)
. With probability at least
1-\eta
,

\max_{s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\|P(\cdot\mid s,a)-\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2S\log(2)+2\log(SA/\eta)}{n}}.

Moreover, if the left-hand side is at most
\delta
, then the induced
N
-armed kernels satisfy

\max_{\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}^{N},\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}^{N}}\left\|\bm{P}(\cdot\mid\bm{s},\bm{a})-\widehat{\bm{P}}(\cdot\mid\bm{s},\bm{a})\right\|_{1}\leq N\delta.

And for any policy
\pi
, the induced transition matrices
\bm{P}^{\pi},\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\pi}
satisfy
\left\lVert\bm{P}^{\pi}-\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\pi}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq N\delta.

We now state Lemma
7.2
and Lemma
7.3
.

Lemma 7.2

(Bound on
\rho^{\mathrm{rel}}-\widehat{\rho}^{\pi_{\mathrm{TS}}}(\bm{s}_{0})
)

Consider an
N
-armed RB problem with an initial state
\bm{s}_{0}
satisfying Assumptions
4.2
and
4.2
. Suppose

N\geq\max\left\{\frac{4}{\min\{y^{\star}(\tilde{s},0),y^{\star}(\tilde{s},1)\}},\left(\frac{4M}{\gamma}\right)^{2}\right\}

and the single-armed model accuracy satisfies

\max_{s\in\mathcal{S},\ a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\|\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)-P(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{1}\leq\delta,

where
\gamma
is defined in (
57
) and
M
is defined in (
59
). Then

\rho^{\mathrm{rel}}-\widehat{\rho}^{\pi_{\mathrm{TS}}}(\bm{s}_{0})\leq C_{6}N\delta+6\exp(8)S\widetilde{V}_{\max}\exp(-CN),

where
\widetilde{V}_{\max}
is defined in (
55
), and
C_{6},C
are constants independent of
N
.

Lemma 7.3

(Bound on
\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}(\bm{s}_{0})
)

Consider an
N
-armed RB problem with an initial state
\bm{s}_{0}
satisfying Assumptions
4.2
and
4.2
. Suppose

N\geq\max\left\{\frac{4}{\min\{y^{\star}(\tilde{s},0),y^{\star}(\tilde{s},1)\}},\left(\frac{4\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{up}}}{\widehat{\gamma}^{\mathrm{low}}}\right)^{2}\right\}

and the single-armed model accuracy satisfies

\max_{s\in\mathcal{S},\ a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\|\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)-P(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{1}\leq\delta\leq\delta_{\min},

where
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{up}}
is defined in (
69
),
\widehat{\gamma}^{\mathrm{low}}
is defined in (
66
), and
\delta_{\min}
is defined in (
14
). Then

\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}(\bm{s}_{0})\leq C_{7}N\delta+6\exp(8)S\widehat{\widetilde{V}}_{\max}\exp(-\widehat{C}N),

where
\widehat{\widetilde{V}}_{\max}
is defined in (
67
), and
C_{7},\widehat{C}
are constants independent of
N
.

Lemma
7.2
is proved in Appendix
G.1
. Here we provide a proof outline of Lemma
7.3
,
which is the part where the perturbation analysis enters the proof of
Theorem
4.2
. The complete proof is deferred to
Appendix
G.2
. Unlike
Lemma
7.2
, which analyzes the true two-set policy,
Lemma
7.3
analyzes the learned two-set policy

\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}
under the true model. Therefore, before applying the Lyapunov
framework, we must first verify that the empirical LP solution preserves
the structural properties required by the two-set policy and its Lyapunov
analysis. We use Theorem
4.3
exactly for this purpose.

Proof outline of Lemma
7.3
.

Step 1: Structural stability from the perturbation theorem.

Since the single-armed model accuracy satisfies the requirements of Theorem
4.3
, the empirical LP
preserves the key structure of the true LP. Therefore, the empirical two-set objects used below,
including
\widehat{g}
and
\widehat{V}
, are well-defined.

Step 2: Empirical Lyapunov analysis.

We now apply the two-set Lyapunov analysis to the empirical system. Let

\Sigma_{t}=(\bm{X}_{t},D_{t}^{\mathrm{OL}},D_{t}^{\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}})

denote the augmented state of the learned two-set policy, and let

\widehat{V}
be the empirical Lyapunov function constructed from

\widehat{y}^{\star}
,
\widehat{\mu}^{\star}
, and the perturbed local
stability matrix
\widehat{\Phi}
. The empirical two-set policy admits the following drift and
gap-dominance bounds, stated formally as
Lemmas
G.20
and
G.21
,
respectively. Informally, for every augmented state
\sigma
,

(\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}-I)\widehat{V}(\sigma)\leq-\frac{\widehat{\gamma}}{4(\widehat{\beta}+r_{\max})}\widehat{g}(\sigma)+6\exp(8)S\widehat{\widetilde{V}}_{\max}\exp(-\widehat{C}N),

and

\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}}-r^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}(\sigma)\leq\widehat{g}(\sigma).

Thus, in the notation of the Lyapunov framework, the empirical Lyapunov
function
\widehat{V}
satisfies Conditions
(C1)

and
(C2)
with

F(\sigma)=\widehat{g}(\sigma),C_{0}=\frac{\widehat{\gamma}}{4(\widehat{\beta}+r_{\max})},G_{1}(N)=6\exp(8)S\widehat{\widetilde{V}}_{\max}\exp(-\widehat{C}N),C_{0}^{\prime}=1,G_{2}(N)=0.

The perturbation theorem is used here to ensure that the hatted objects
appearing in these bounds are well-defined and that the empirical
constants are uniformly controlled.

Step 3: Drift transfer.

The drift bound in Step 2 is for the empirical transition kernel

\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}
, while Lemma
7.3
concerns
the performance of
\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}
under the true transition kernel

\bm{P}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}
. We therefore apply
Lemma
6.7
with

Q=\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}},P=\bm{P}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}},R=\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}},F=\widehat{g},V=\widehat{V},\bar{r}=r^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}.

Using the values of
C_{0},C_{0}^{\prime},G_{1}(N),G_{2}(N)
from Step 2, we obtain

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}(\bm{s}_{0})\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4(\widehat{\beta}+r_{\max})}{\widehat{\gamma}}\sup_{\sigma}\left|(\bm{P}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}-\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}})\widehat{V}(\sigma)\right|+\frac{4(\widehat{\beta}+r_{\max})}{\widehat{\gamma}}6\exp(8)S\widehat{\widetilde{V}}_{\max}\exp(-\widehat{C}N).

The model-error term is upper bounded by Lemma
G.32
:

\sup_{\sigma}\left|(\bm{P}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}-\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}})\widehat{V}(\sigma)\right|\leq\widehat{V}_{\max}N\delta.

Substituting this bound into the display from Step 3 gives

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}(\bm{s}_{0})\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4\widehat{V}_{\max}(\widehat{\beta}+r_{\max})}{\widehat{\gamma}}N\delta+\frac{4(\widehat{\beta}+r_{\max})}{\widehat{\gamma}}6\exp(8)S\widehat{\widetilde{V}}_{\max}\exp(-\widehat{C}N).

Step 4: Replacing empirical constants by deterministic bounds.

Finally, Theorem
4.3
and the auxiliary well-definedness
lemmas provide deterministic bounds on the empirical constants:

\widehat{\gamma}\geq\widehat{\gamma}^{\mathrm{low}},\widehat{\beta}\leq\widehat{\beta}^{\max},\|\widehat{V}\|_{\infty}\leq\widehat{V}_{\max},\widehat{\widetilde{V}}_{\max}<\infty.

These bounds depend only on the original single-armed RB parameters, not on

N,n,\eta
, or the initial state. Hence the previous display implies

\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}(\bm{s}_{0})\leq C_{7}N\delta+6\exp(8)S\widehat{\widetilde{V}}_{\max}\exp(-\widehat{C}N),

for a constant
C_{7}
independent of
N,n,\eta
, and
\bm{s}_{0}
. This
is Lemma
7.3
.

Completing the proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Combining Lemma
7.2
and Lemma
7.3
yields Theorem
4.2
once we relate the single-armed model accuracy parameter
\delta
to the sample size
n
under the generative model.
First, we notice that when

n\leq\left(2S\log 2+2\log(SA/\eta)\right)/\delta_{\min}^{2},

where
\delta_{\min}
is defined in (
14
), we can choose
C^{\prime}=2/\delta_{\min}
, then

\rho^{\star}(\bm{s}_{0})-\rho^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{TS}}}(\bm{s}_{0})\leq 1\leq C^{\prime}N\sqrt{\frac{S+\log(SA/\eta)}{n}}+C^{\prime\prime}\exp(-C_{5}N).

Therefore, we only need to consider the case when

n\geq\left(2S\log 2+2\log(SA/\eta)\right)/\delta_{\min}^{2},

In this case, according to Lemma
6.1
, we have the single-armed model accuracy:

\max_{s\in\mathcal{S},\ a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\|\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)-P(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{1}\leq\delta\triangleq\sqrt{\frac{2S\log(2)+2\log(SA/\eta)}{n}}\leq\delta_{\min},

with probability at least
1-\eta
. We consider the case when

N\geq N_{\max}\triangleq\max\left\{\frac{4}{\min\{y^{\star}(\tilde{s},0),y^{\star}(\tilde{s},1)\}},\left(\frac{4M}{\gamma}\right)^{2},\left(\frac{4\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{up}}}{\widehat{\gamma}^{\mathrm{low}}}\right)^{2}\right\},

(23)

where
M
is defined in (
59
),
\gamma
in (
57
),
\widehat{M}^{\mathrm{up}}
in (
69
) and
\widehat{\gamma}^{\mathrm{low}}
in (
66
). Based on these conditions, both the conditions of
N
and
\delta
in Lemmas
7.2
and
7.3
hold. Therefore, we can combine the results of these two lemmas by choosing

C_{3}=C_{6}+C_{7},C_{4}=6\exp(8)S(\tilde{V}_{\max}+\widehat{\tilde{V}}_{\max})\text{ and }C_{5}=\min\{C,\widehat{C}\},

which completes the proof.

8
Proof of Theorem
4.3

In this section we provide the proof of Theorem
4.3
, which will be preceded by many intermediate lemmas, organized into three subsections
8.2
,
8.3
,
8.4
and
8.5
. We firstly give a proof outline in
8.1
to show the high-level ideas.

8.1
Proof outline

The proof uses a primal-dual perturbation argument. Under the
nondegeneracy and strict-gap assumptions, a small perturbation of the
transition kernel should not change which LP constraints are active. We
therefore build candidate primal and dual solutions for the perturbed LP
using the same active structure as in the true LP. We then check primal
feasibility, dual feasibility, complementary slackness, and uniqueness.

One complication is that the RB LP has a redundant flow-balance constraint,
and the dual bias vector is only unique up to an additive constant. For
this reason, we first perturb the state occupancy instead of perturbing
the primal and dual variables directly. Around the optimal state occupancy

\mu^{\star}
, the locally linear policy follows
\bar{\pi}^{\star}
at all
non-neutral states and uses the neutral state
\tilde{s}
to satisfy the
budget. This gives the local linearization matrix

U=P^{\bar{\pi}^{\star}}-(c^{\bar{\pi}^{\star}}-\alpha\mathbf{1})\xi
, where

\xi=P_{\tilde{s}1}-P_{\tilde{s}0}
. Replacing
P
by
\widehat{P}
gives
the perturbed matrix
\widehat{U}
.

We define
H_{U}=(I-\Phi)^{-1}-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}
and

\widehat{\mu}=\mu^{\star}\bigl(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U}\bigr)^{-1}.

The matrix
H_{U}
measures how sensitive the state occupancy is to the
model perturbation. The key identity is

\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{\star}=\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U}.

This is similar to the usual perturbation formula for stationary
distributions of Markov chains, although
U
and
\widehat{U}
are not
transition matrices.

After constructing
\widehat{\mu}
, we use a linear map
W
to define the
primal candidate
\widehat{y}=\widehat{\mu}W
. The construction of
W
ensures that

\widehat{y}
satisfies the perturbed budget, flow-balance, and normalization
constraints. It also preserves the support pattern: active entries of

y^{\star}
remain positive, while inactive entries remain zero.
For the dual side, we construct
(\widehat{\zeta},\widehat{\lambda},\widehat{h})
so that
the perturbed dual constraints are tight on the active state-action pairs.
The dual perturbation bounds then show that inactive constraints still
have positive slack. Thus the constructed primal and dual candidates are
feasible and satisfy complementary slackness, so they are optimal. The
strict slack on inactive actions also gives uniqueness and support
preservation.

Finally, support preservation implies that the empirical optimal
single-arm policy can differ from the true one only at the neutral state.
Together with a Markov-chain perturbation bound, this gives the
aperiodicity, irreducibility, and mixing-time conclusion in the theorem.

8.2
Candidate state occupancy construction

Throughout the proof, fix an arbitrary policy
\pi^{\star}
which satisfies that
\pi^{\star}(s)=\bar{\pi}^{\star}(s)
for all
s\neq\tilde{s}
. It is straightforward to check that

P^{\bar{\pi}^{\star}}-(c^{\bar{\pi}^{\star}}-\alpha\mathbf{1})\xi=P^{\pi^{\star}}-(c^{\pi^{\star}}-\alpha\mathbf{1})\xi

where for any policy
\pi
,
c^{\pi}(s)=\pi(1\mid s)
, and
\xi
is the row vector
P_{\tilde{s}1}-P_{\tilde{s}0}
. We then define the local linearization matrix
U
and its perturbed analogue

\displaystyle U=P^{\pi^{\star}}-(c^{\pi^{\star}}-\alpha\mathbf{1})\xi,\qquad\widehat{U}=\widehat{P}^{\pi^{\star}}-(c^{\pi^{\star}}-\alpha\mathbf{1})\widehat{\xi},

where
\widehat{\xi}\triangleq\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}1}-\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}0}
. We also define
H_{U}=(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}.

Note that
U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}=\Phi
, so the inverse
(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}
exists due to the local stability condition of Assumption
4.2
.
Finally we define the candidate state occupancy distribution

\widehat{\mu}\triangleq\mu^{\star}(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})^{-1}.

(24)

Such a definition requires the invertibility of
(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})^{-1}
, so it suffices for now to require that we have
\left\lVert(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}<1
.
This definition is motivated by the stationary distribution perturbation identity for Markov chains
(
Meyer 1975
)
and we can now verify that an analogous identity holds in our case:

Lemma 8.1

Suppose
(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu))
and
(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})
are both invertible. Then

\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{\star}=\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U}.

Proof 8.2

Proof
Right-multiplying both sides of (
24
) by
(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})
, we have that

\mu^{\star}=\widehat{\mu}(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})=\widehat{\mu}-\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U},

which can be directly rearranged to obtain the desired equality.

Given this definition of
\widehat{\mu}
and Lemma
8.1
, we can now also define the perturbed version of
H_{U}
:
H_{\widehat{U}}=(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}.

This definition requires the invertibility of
(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))
, which is provided by the following lemma.

Lemma 8.3

Suppose that
\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}<1
and

\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{1-\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}}{1+\frac{\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\star}_{\min}}}}.

(25)

Then

1-\left\lVert\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\geq\frac{1-\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}}{2}>0

and thus
(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))
is invertible.

Lemma
8.3
is proved in
E.3
and we encourage interested readers to read
E.2
for additional properties of
U,\widehat{U},\mu^{\star},\widehat{\mu},H_{U}
, and
H_{\widehat{U}}
.

8.3
Perturbed primal LP solution construction

We next construct candidate solutions for the primal and dual perturbed
LPs (
11
) and (
12
), and then verify
their feasibility and optimality.

We first construct the candidate solution for the perturbed primal LP. By
the constraint (
8b
) of the true primal LP,
we have
y^{\star}\tilde{e}=\alpha
. Moreover, by the nondegeneracy
assumption, for all
s\neq\tilde{s}
,

y^{\star}(s,a)=\mu^{\star}(s)\mathbb{I}\{\pi^{\star}(a\mid s)=1\}
. Therefore,
we can reconstruct
y^{\star}
from
\mu^{\star}
by using the form of

\pi^{\star}
, and then use
y^{\star}\tilde{e}=\alpha
to determine

y^{\star}(\tilde{s},0)
and
y^{\star}(\tilde{s},1)
. Hence
y^{\star}

satisfies

\displaystyle y^{\star}(s,a)

\displaystyle=\mu^{\star}(s)\pi^{\star}(a\mid s)\quad\forall s\neq\tilde{s},

\displaystyle y^{\star}(\tilde{s},1)

\displaystyle=\alpha-\sum_{s\neq\tilde{s}}\pi^{\star}(1\mid s)\mu^{\star}(s)=\alpha\left(\mu^{\star}\mathbf{1}\right)-\sum_{s\neq\tilde{s}}\pi^{\star}(1\mid s)\mu^{\star}(s),

\displaystyle y^{\star}(\tilde{s},0)

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)-\sum_{s\neq\tilde{s}}\pi^{\star}(0\mid s)\mu^{\star}(s)=(1-\alpha)\left(\mu^{\star}\mathbf{1}\right)-\sum_{s\neq\tilde{s}}\pi^{\star}(0\mid s)\mu^{\star}(s).

We wrote the last two equations using
1=\mu^{\star}\mathbf{1}
so that the
above display defines a linear system with matrix
W
satisfying

y^{\star}=\mu^{\star}W
. Using the same linear map, we define the candidate
solution
\widehat{y}
by
\widehat{y}=\widehat{\mu}W
. This construction also ensures
that
\widehat{y}(s,a)=0
for all
(s,a)
such that
y^{\star}(s,a)=0
.
The matrix
W
will play a key role in all of our subsequent analysis. Clearly its definition is closely related to the assumed form of
\mu^{\star}
and
y^{\star}
.
The following lemma guarantees the feasibility of
\widehat{y}
and is proved in
E.3
.

Lemma 8.4

Suppose that
\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}<1
and
that (
25
) holds. Then
\widehat{y}=\widehat{\mu}W
satisfies

\left\|\widehat{y}-y^{\star}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}.

(26)

Furthermore, if

\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\min_{i:y_{i}^{\star}>0}\frac{y_{i}^{\star}}{2\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}},

(27)

then
\widehat{y}\geq y^{\star}/2
elementwise and
\widehat{y}
is feasible for the
perturbed primal LP (
11
). In particular, the support
of
\widehat{y}
agrees with the support of
y^{\star}
.

8.4
Perturbed dual LP solution construction

Next we construct the candidate dual solution
(\widehat{\zeta},\widehat{\lambda},\widehat{h})
. We note that the complementary slackness property (
10
) of the true LP, combined with the assumed form of
y^{\star}
, implies that for all
i=(s,a)
such that
y_{i}^{\star}>0
we have

\displaystyle r(i)+\lambda\tilde{e}_{i}+P_{i}h=\zeta+h(s),

(28)

which will be used to guide the candidate dual construction. The following lemma gives a more explicit form of the solution to the true dual LP which will eventually prove useful, and also motivates the candidate perturbed dual solution which will be constructed below.

Lemma 8.5

There exists some
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
such that

h^{\star}=H_{U}Wr+\kappa\mathbf{1}.

Lemma
8.5
is proved in
E.4
. We now construct the candidate dual solution. Motivated by
Lemma
8.5
, define

\widehat{h}=H_{\widehat{U}}Wr.

It remains to define
\widehat{\lambda}
and
\widehat{\zeta}
. Since (
28
) holds for both
(\tilde{s},0)
and
(\tilde{s},1)
, we have the “equal Q-function” property for the neutral state that

r(\tilde{s},0)+P_{\tilde{s}0}h^{\star}=r(\tilde{s},1)+\lambda^{\star}+P_{\tilde{s}1}h^{\star}.

We choose
\widehat{\lambda}
so that the same indifference holds for the
perturbed dual candidate:

r(\tilde{s},0)+\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}0}\widehat{h}=r(\tilde{s},1)+\widehat{\lambda}+\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}1}\widehat{h}.

Equivalently, we set

\displaystyle\widehat{\lambda}=r(\tilde{s},0)-r(\tilde{s},1)-\widehat{\xi}\widehat{h}.

(29)

Finally, as noted in the proof of Lemma
8.5
, the equality of the objective values for the optimal solutions to the primal and dual LPs implies that
\zeta^{\star}=\mu^{\star}Wr+\lambda^{\star}\alpha
, so we define
\widehat{\zeta}
as

\displaystyle\widehat{\zeta}=\widehat{\mu}Wr+\widehat{\lambda}\alpha,

(30)

so that the perturbed dual objective matches the objective value of the
candidate primal solution.

Now having defined
(\widehat{\zeta},\widehat{\lambda},\widehat{h})
, we find conditions for their dual feasibility. The only constraint which we need to check is (
12b
). We check each row of (
12b
), dividing them into two cases based on whether the constraint will hold with equality or inequality. First we handle the equality case.

Lemma 8.6

Suppose that
\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}<1
and that (
25
) holds. Then we have

r^{\pi^{\star}}+\widehat{P}^{\pi^{\star}}\widehat{h}+\widehat{\lambda}c^{\pi^{\star}}=\widehat{h}+\widehat{\zeta}\mathbf{1}

and

r(\tilde{s},0)+\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}0}\widehat{h}=r(\tilde{s},1)+\widehat{\lambda}+\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}1}\widehat{h}=\widehat{h}(\tilde{s})+\widehat{\zeta}.

This lemma shows that the dual constraints are active on the support of
y^{\star}
.
However, this is not yet enough for dual feasibility. We still need to
check that the dual constraints also hold for the inactive pairs

(s,a)
with
y^{\star}(s,a)=0
. The next lemma
shows that the dual variables only change by a small amount, so dual constraints still have positive slacks after perturbation.

Lemma 8.7

Suppose that
\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}<1
and
that (
25
) holds. Then

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{h}-h^{\star}\right\|_{\textnormal{span}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\|h^{\star}\right\|_{\textnormal{span}}}{1-\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}},

\displaystyle|\widehat{\lambda}-\lambda^{\star}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\xi-\widehat{\xi}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\|h^{\star}\right\|_{\textnormal{span}}+\left\|\widehat{h}-h^{\star}\right\|_{\textnormal{span}},

\displaystyle|\widehat{\zeta}-\zeta^{\star}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\|h^{\star}\right\|_{\textnormal{span}}+|\widehat{\lambda}-\lambda^{\star}|.

Furthermore, if the strict-gap perturbation condition in
Theorem
4.3
holds, then for every

(s,a)
such that
y^{\star}(s,a)=0
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\zeta}+\widehat{h}(s)-r(s,a)-\widehat{\lambda}\mathbb{I}(a=1)-\widehat{P}_{sa}\widehat{h}\geq\frac{1}{2}\left[\zeta^{\star}+h^{\star}(s)-r(s,a)-\lambda^{\star}\mathbb{I}(a=1)-P_{sa}h^{\star}\right].

The first part of the lemma controls how much the dual variables move.
The last inequality shows that every inactive pair
(s,a)
still has
positive dual slack after perturbation.
Combining this with Lemma
8.6
, the perturbed dual constraints are tight on
the support of
y^{\star}
and strictly slack outside the support.
Hence the candidate dual solution is feasible, and together with the
primal solution from Section
8.3
, we obtain these candidates have the same objective
value and satisfy complementary slackness. Therefore they are optimal for
the perturbed primal and dual LPs. Lemma
8.6
and Lemma
8.7
are proved in
E.5
.

8.5
Completing the proof of Theorem
4.3

First we confirm the optimality of the constructed candidate solutions
\widehat{y}
and
(\widehat{\zeta},\widehat{\lambda},\widehat{h})
for their respective LPs.
Lemma
8.4
guarantees primal feasibility of
\widehat{y}
, and Lemmas
8.6
and
8.7
guarantee dual feasibility of
(\widehat{\zeta},\widehat{\lambda},\widehat{h})
. Since by construction these primal and dual solutions have the same objective value of
\widehat{\mu}Wr=\widehat{\zeta}-\alpha\widehat{\lambda}
, they are both optimal.
The next lemma shows the uniqueness of both the primal and dual solutions and is proved in
E.6
.

Lemma 8.8

The primal candidate solution
\widehat{y}
and dual candidate solutions
(\widehat{\zeta},\widehat{\lambda},\widehat{h})
are unique.

The following lemma which is proved in
E.7
checks the perturbation bounds from the statement of the theorem and confirm that all assumptions are satisfied for all the lemmas used in the proof.

Lemma 8.9

Under the conditions of Theorem
4.3
,
the perturbation bounds in Part 4 hold.
Moreover, the technical conditions (
25
) and
(
27
) used in the construction are satisfied.

Now it remains to prove the Markov-chain conclusion.
which is guaranteed by the following lemma and its proof is contained in
E.8
.

Lemma 8.10

Under the conditions of Theorem
4.3
,
the single-arm Markov chain induced by
\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}
under
\widehat{P}
is
aperiodic and irreducible. Its stationary distribution is

\widehat{\mu}^{\star}(s)=\widehat{y}^{\star}(s,0)+\widehat{y}^{\star}(s,1),

and its mixing time is at most
(3+\log_{2}S)\tau
.

We now match the numbered conclusions in the theorem.
Part 1 follows from the support preservation in
Lemma
8.4
. Since the support is unchanged, the
neutral state
\tilde{s}
is also preserved.
Part 2 is the lower bound

\widehat{y}\geq y^{\star}/2
from Lemma
8.4
.
Part 3 follows from
Lemma
8.3
. After support preservation, the
perturbed locally linear policy is the one induced by
\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}
, so

\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}
is the perturbed local-stability matrix
\widehat{\Phi}
.
Part 4 follows from
Lemma
8.4
and
Lemma
8.7
, together with

\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq 3\delta,\left\lVert\widehat{\xi}-\xi\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq 2\delta.

Part 5 is the strict-gap conclusion in
Lemma
8.7
, after using the support preservation from Part 1. Finally,
Part 6 is
Lemma
8.10
. This proves all parts of
Theorem
4.3
.

Appendix A
Additional Notation

Basic notation.

Let
\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathbb{N}_{+}
denote the sets of real numbers, nonnegative integers, and positive integers, respectively.
For
n\leq n^{\prime}
with
n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, define
[n]\triangleq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and
[n:n^{\prime}]\triangleq\{n,n+1,\dots,n^{\prime}\}
.
We also define the grid
[0,1]_{n}\triangleq\left\{\frac{i}{n}\ \middle|\ i\in\mathbb{N},\ 0\leq\frac{i}{n}\leq 1\right\}
.
For vectors
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{S}
, we use the inner product
\langle u,v\rangle\triangleq\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}u(s)v(s)
.

Asymptotic notation.

We use standard Bachmann–Landau notation
O(\cdot)
,
\Omega(\cdot)
,
\Theta(\cdot)
,
o(\cdot)
, and
\omega(\cdot)
.
We also use
\widetilde{O}(\cdot)
,
\widetilde{\Omega}(\cdot)
, and
\widetilde{\Theta}(\cdot)
to hide logarithmic factors in problem parameters such as
n
,
N
,
S
,
A
, and
1/\eta
.

Symbol

Meaning

\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}

State and Action space of each arm

S,A

Cardinality of the state and action space,
S=|\mathcal{S}|,A=|\mathcal{A}|

s,a

one-dimension state and action

\bm{s},\bm{a}

N
-dimension state and action

P(\cdot\mid s,a)

True transition probability given
(s,a)
for single arm

\widehat{P}(\cdot\mid s,a)

Estimated transition probability given
(s,a)
for single arm

\bm{P}(\cdot\mid\bm{s},\bm{a})

True transition probability given
(\bm{s},\bm{a})
for the
N
-arm system

\widehat{\bm{P}}(\cdot\mid\bm{s},\bm{a})

Estimated transition probability given
(\bm{s},\bm{a})
for the
N
-armed system

\bar{\pi}^{\star}

Optimal single-armed policy for the true system

\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}

Optimal single-armed policy for the empirical system

\mu^{\star}

Stationary distribution obtained by running
\bar{\pi}^{\star}
under the true MDP

\widehat{\mu}^{\star}

Stationary distribution obtained by running
\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}
under the empirical MDP

One-step transition matrix induced by a policy
\pi

Limiting transition matrix of the Markov chain under policy
\pi

Appendix B
Common Technical Lemmas

This section collects the proof of Lemma
6.7
together with several
finite-state Markov-chain perturbation bounds used later in the proofs.

Proof B.1

Proof of Lemma
6.7
.
Fix an arbitrary initial state
z_{0}
, and let

\Delta:=\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}|[(P-Q)V](z)|
. Since
\mathcal{Z}
is
finite, for every function
f:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
, the Cesaro average

T^{-1}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}[f(Z_{t})\mid Z_{0}=z_{0}]
exists.

For every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, we have

(P-I)V(z)=(Q-I)V(z)+(P-Q)V(z)\leq-C_{0}F(z)+G_{1}(N)+\Delta
. Averaging this
inequality along the Markov chain governed by
P
, we obtain, for every

T\geq 1
,

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{P}[V(Z_{T})\mid Z_{0}=z_{0}]-V(z_{0})}{T}\leq-C_{0}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}[F(Z_{t})\mid Z_{0}=z_{0}]+G_{1}(N)+\Delta.

Since
\mathcal{Z}
is finite,
V
is bounded, so

(\mathbb{E}_{P}[V(Z_{T})\mid Z_{0}=z_{0}]-V(z_{0}))/T\to 0
. Rearranging the previous
display and taking limits gives

\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}[F(Z_{t})\mid Z_{0}=z_{0}]\leq\frac{G_{1}(N)+\Delta}{C_{0}}.

(31)

Next, averaging
R-\bar{r}(z)\leq C_{0}^{\prime}F(z)+G_{2}(N)
along the same trajectory
gives, for every
T\geq 1
,

R-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}[\bar{r}(Z_{t})\mid Z_{0}=z_{0}]\leq C_{0}^{\prime}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}[F(Z_{t})\mid Z_{0}=z_{0}]+G_{2}(N).

Taking limits on both sides yields

R-\rho_{P}(z_{0})\leq C_{0}^{\prime}\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{P}[F(Z_{t})\mid Z_{0}=z_{0}]+G_{2}(N).

Combining this inequality with (
31
) we can get the desired result.

Lemma B.2

Let
Q,\widetilde{Q}
be two Markov transition matrices on the same finite state space
\mathcal{Z}
. Suppose that the chain
Q
is unichain, aperiodic, and has mixing time bounded by
\tau
. Then if
\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}-Q\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\frac{1}{4|\mathcal{Z}|\tau(5+\log_{2}|\mathcal{Z}|)}
,
the Markov chain
\widetilde{Q}
is unichain, aperiodic, and has mixing time bounded by
(3+\log_{2}|\mathcal{Z}|)\tau
.

Proof B.3

Proof.
Let
Q^{\infty}=\mathbf{1}\mu^{\top}
for some
\mu
, which is a valid expression since
Q
is unichain and aperiodic. Suppose
\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}-Q\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\delta
. Let
t\geq\tau
be a positive integer which we will choose later.
By triangle inequality

\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{t}-Q^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{t}-Q^{t}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}+\left\lVert Q^{t}-Q^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}.

Now we bound each of these terms. First,

\widetilde{Q}^{t}-Q^{t}=\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\widetilde{Q}^{t-1-k}(\widetilde{Q}-Q)Q^{k},

and so

\displaystyle\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{t}-Q^{t}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{t-1-k}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}-Q\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert Q^{k}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{t-1}1\cdot\delta\cdot 1\leq t\delta

since each
\widetilde{Q}^{t-k}Q^{k-1}
(for
k=1,\dots,t
) is a stochastic matrix and thus has
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}
equal to
1
.
By Lemma
B.4
we have

\left\lVert Q^{t}-Q^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq 2^{-\lfloor t/\tau\rfloor}.

Combining the above two results, we have

\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{t}-Q^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq t\delta+2^{-\lfloor t/\tau\rfloor}<t\delta+2^{-t/\tau+1}.

We can choose
t=(3+\log_{2}|\mathcal{Z}|)\tau
,
\delta=\frac{1}{2|\mathcal{Z}|\tau(3+\log_{2}|\mathcal{Z}|)}
so that
\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{t}-Q^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}<\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}|}
. Then, notice
Q^{\infty}=\mathbf{1}\mu^{\top}
, we have
\max_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\mu(z)\geq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}|}
by a standard argument. Let
z^{\star}
have
\mu(z^{\star})\geq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}|}
. Then for all states
z_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle e_{z_{0}}^{\top}\widetilde{Q}^{t}e_{z^{\star}}

\displaystyle\geq e_{z_{0}}Q^{\infty}e_{z^{\star}}-e_{z_{0}}^{\top}(Q^{\infty}-\widetilde{Q}^{t})e_{z^{\star}}

\displaystyle\geq e_{z_{0}}\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}e_{z^{\star}}-\left\lVert e_{z_{0}}^{\top}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert Q^{\infty}-\widetilde{Q}^{t}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\|e_{z^{\star}}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\mu(z^{\star})-\left\lVert Q^{\infty}-\widetilde{Q}^{t}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}>\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}|}-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{Z}|}=0.

Therefore we have shown that for any
z_{0}
, there is positive probability of reaching
z^{\star}
after
t
steps. Therefore if
z_{0}
is recurrent, then
z^{\star}
is also recurrent and they are in the same minimal closed recurrent class. Thus all recurrent classes contain
z^{\star}
, so there is only one recurrent class, and thus by definition
\widetilde{Q}
is unichain.

Next we bound
\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{t}-\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}
.
By triangle inequality

\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{t}-\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{t}-Q^{t}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}+\left\lVert Q^{t}-Q^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}+\left\lVert Q^{\infty}-\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}.

We have already bounded the first two terms.
For the third term we have

\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}-Q^{\infty}=\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}\widetilde{Q}-Q^{\infty}Q=\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}(\widetilde{Q}-Q)+(\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}-Q^{\infty})Q,

which implies

(\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}-Q^{\infty})(I-Q)=\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}(\widetilde{Q}-Q).

Right-multiplying by
H_{Q}
we have

\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}(\widetilde{Q}-Q)H_{Q}=(\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}-Q^{\infty})(I-Q)H_{Q}=(\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}-Q^{\infty})(I-Q^{\infty})=\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}-Q^{\infty}

using the fact that
\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}Q^{\infty}=\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}\mu^{\top}=\mathbf{1}\mu^{\top}=Q^{\infty}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left\lVert Q^{\infty}-\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}(\widetilde{Q}-Q)H_{Q}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}-Q\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert H_{Q}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}-Q\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}2\tau\leq 2\delta\tau

using Lemma
B.6
in the penultimate inequality.
Therefore, we have

\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}^{t}-\widetilde{Q}^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq t\delta+2^{-\lfloor\frac{t}{\tau}\rfloor}+2\delta\tau<(t+2\tau)\delta+2^{-\frac{t}{\tau}+1}.

By choosing
t=(3+\log_{2}|\mathcal{Z}|)\tau,\delta=\frac{1}{4(5+\log_{2}|\mathcal{Z}|)\tau}
we can make this
\leq\frac{1}{2}
, which implies that the standard mixing time of
\widetilde{Q}
is within
(3+\log_{2}|\mathcal{Z}|)\tau
. Then it implies that
\widetilde{Q}
is aperiodic. In conclusion, if
\left\lVert\widetilde{Q}-Q\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\frac{1}{4|\mathcal{Z}|\tau(5+\log_{2}|\mathcal{Z}|)}
, the Markov chain
\widetilde{Q}
is unichain and aperiodic and the mixing time of
\widetilde{Q}
is bounded by
(3+\log_{2}|\mathcal{Z}|)\tau
.

Lemma B.4

Suppose that the Markov chain
Q
is unichain, aperiodic, and has mixing time bounded by
\tau
. Then for any time
t\geq\tau
,

\left\lVert Q^{t}-Q^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq 2^{-\lfloor t/\tau\rfloor}.

Proof B.5

Proof.
Denote
\mu
as the stationary distribution of the Markov chain
Q
. Hence
Q^{\infty}=\mathbf{1}\mu
. By the definition of the uniform mixing time
\tau
, we have

\|Q^{\tau}-Q^{\infty}\|_{\infty\to\infty}=\max_{s}\sum_{j}|Q^{\tau}(s,j)-\mu(j)|=2\max_{s}\|Q^{\tau}(s,\cdot)-\mu(\cdot)\|_{\text{TV}}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Let
t=k\,\tau+r,0\leq r<\tau.
Then

Q^{t}-Q^{\infty}=Q^{r}\bigl(Q^{k\,\tau}-Q^{\infty}\bigr).
Hence,
\forall t\geq\tau
,

\left\lVert Q^{t}-Q^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\left\lVert Q^{k\tau}-Q^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\left\lVert Q^{\tau}-Q^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}^{k}\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}=2^{-\lfloor t/\tau\rfloor}.

Lemma B.6

Let
Q
be a finite-state Markov transition matrix. If
Q
is
unichain and aperiodic and has mixing time bounded by
\tau
, then
the deviation matrix of
Q
, denoted as
H_{Q}
, satisfies

\left\lVert H_{Q}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq 2\tau.

Proof B.7

Proof
Since
Q
is aperiodic (so the Cesaro limit in the expression for

H_{Q}
coincides with the usual limit), we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert H_{Q}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\left(Q^{t}-Q^{\infty}\right)\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\left\lVert Q^{t}-Q^{\infty}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}2^{-\lfloor\frac{t}{\tau}\rfloor}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}2^{-\lfloor\frac{\tau k+j}{\tau}\rfloor}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{\tau-1}2^{-\lfloor\frac{\tau k}{\tau}\rfloor}=\tau\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-k}=2\tau.

Appendix C
Preliminaries for the proof of Theorem
4.1

Theorem
4.1
is based on the ID policy, proposed by
Zhang et al. 2025
. To facilitate the presentation of the proof of Theorem
4.1
, in this section we provide some preliminaries on the ID policy and its analysis. The content is mostly taken from
Zhang et al. 2025
. We present the description of ID policy in Section
C.1
, the analysis of ID policy in Section
C.2
, and the empirical ID policy in Section
C.3
.

C.1
Description of the ID policy

In this subsection, we describe the ID policy and the corresponding ID reassignment subroutine.

ID policy execution (ID-based prioritization).

Each arm is assigned a unique
ID
in
\{1,\dots,N\}
(after reassignment described below).
At each time
t
, the policy first samples an
ideal action

A^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{i,t}\sim\bar{\pi}_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid S_{i,t}),\quad\forall i\in[N].

It then attempts to execute these ideal actions in ascending ID order.
Define the conforming number
N_{t}^{\star}
, i.e.,
the number of arms that actually conform to their ideal actions at time
t
:

N_{t}^{\star}=\max\left\{n\in[N]:\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{k,i}(S_{i,t},A^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{i,t})\leq\alpha_{k}N,\quad\forall k\in[K]\right\}.

(32)

The realized action is
A_{i,t}=A^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{i,t}
for
i\in[N_{t}^{\star}]
, and
A_{i,t}=0
otherwise. Details of the ID policy is shown in Algorithm
1
.

Input:

N
-armed WCMDP instance and LP relaxation (
2
)

Output:

actions
(A_{i,t})_{i\in[N]}
at each time
t

1

Solve the LP relaxation (
2
) and construct

\{\bar{\pi}_{i}^{\star}\}_{i\in[N]}
.

2

Reassign IDs using Algorithm
2
.

3

for

t=0,1,2,\ldots

do

4

Sample ideal actions

A^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{i,t}\sim\bar{\pi}_{i}^{\star}(\cdot\mid S_{i,t})

for all
i\in[N]
.

5

Compute the conforming number
N_{t}^{\star}
from
(
32
).

6

Set
A_{i,t}=A^{\mathrm{ideal}}_{i,t}
for
i\leq N_{t}^{\star}
, and

A_{i,t}=0
for
i>N_{t}^{\star}
.

Algorithm 1

ID policy with reassignment

Input:

optimal state–action frequencies

\bigl(y_{i}^{\star}(s,a)\bigr)_{i\in[N],\,s\in\mathcal{S},\,a\in\mathcal{A}}
,
budgets
(\alpha_{k})_{k\in[K]}

Output:

new arm IDs recorded in
\mathrm{newID}(i)
for each
i\in[N]

1

Compute
C_{k,i}^{\star}
for all
i\in[N]
,
k\in[K]
, and the active set
\mathcal{A}
.

2

if

\mathcal{A}=\emptyset

then

3

Set
\mathrm{newID}(i)\leftarrow i
for all
i\in[N]
. ;

//
No reassignment needed

else

4

Set
F\leftarrow\emptyset
. ;

//
Arms already assigned new IDs

Set

\alpha_{\min}\leftarrow\min_{k\in[K]}\alpha_{k},\delta\leftarrow\alpha_{\min}/4,d\leftarrow\left\lceil\frac{(c_{\max}-\delta)K}{\alpha_{\min}/2-\delta}\right\rceil.

5

For each
k\in\mathcal{A}
, set

D_{k}\leftarrow\left\{i\in[N]:C_{k,i}^{\star}\geq\delta\right\}.

6

for

\ell=0,1,\ldots,\lfloor N/d\rfloor-1

do

7

Set

\mathcal{I}(\ell)\leftarrow[\ell d+1:(\ell+1)d]

and
j\leftarrow\ell d+1
.

8

foreach

k\in\mathcal{A}

do

9

if

\sum_{i\in F}C_{k,i}^{\star}\mathbf{1}\{\mathrm{newID}(i)\in\mathcal{I}(\ell)\}<\delta

then

10

Pick any
i\in D_{k}
and set

\mathrm{newID}(i)\leftarrow j
.

11

Remove
i
from every
D_{k^{\prime}}
,
k^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
;
set
F\leftarrow F\cup\{i\}
and
j\leftarrow j+1
.

12

For all
i\in[N]\setminus F
, assign
\mathrm{newID}(i)
arbitrarily from

[N]\setminus\{\mathrm{newID}(i^{\prime}):i^{\prime}\in F\}
.

Algorithm 2

ID reassignment

ID reassignment.

In the fully heterogeneous setting, arms can have very different cost profiles under
\bar{\pi}_{i}^{\star}
.
Define the expected type-
k
cost of arm
i
under
\bar{\pi}^{\star}_{i}
as

C_{k,i}^{\star}\triangleq\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S},\,a\in\mathcal{A}}y_{i}^{\star}(s,a)\,c_{k,i}(s,a).

We call constraint
k

active
if
\sum_{i=1}^{N}C_{k,i}^{\star}\geq\frac{\alpha_{k}}{2}N.
Let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq[K]
denote the set of active constraints.

For each subset
D\subseteq[N]
, define the total expected type-
k
cost as

C_{k}^{\star}(D)\triangleq\sum_{i\in D}C_{k,i}^{\star}
, and define the
remaining budget
as

\bar{C}_{k}^{\star}(D)=\alpha_{k}N-C_{k}^{\star}(D)-\frac{\alpha_{k}}{3}|D|\cdot\mathbb{I}(k\notin\mathcal{A}).

The reassignment algorithm is shown in Algorithm
2
. Algorithm
2
permutes IDs so that, as we move along IDs, the remaining budgets

\bar{C}_{k}^{\star}([n])
do not exhibit long “plateaus” (i.e., overly flat cumulative cost),
which would otherwise make
N_{t}^{\star}
overly sensitive to random ideal-action sampling.

C.2
Analysis of the ID policy

The analysis of the ID policy in
Zhang et al. 2025
relies on a specific Lyapunov function. Before we introduce the Lyapunov function, we firstly introduce the one-hot representation of the state and define a key quantity called the focus set.

One-hot representation.

For a system state
\bm{s}=(s_{i})_{i\in[N]}
, let

\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times S}
be its one-hot representation, where

x_{i}(s)=\mathbb{I}\{s_{i}=s\}
. We use
\bm{s}
and
\bm{x}

interchangeably, and write
\bm{X}_{t}
for the one-hot representation of
the stochastic state
\bm{S}_{t}
.

Focus set.

The ID policy executes ideal actions only for a (random) prefix of arms of length
N_{t}^{\star}
.
To analyze this, the paper introduces a
focus set
that tracks a deterministic prefix length selected based on the current system state.

Let
\bm{x}
denote a system state and let
h(\bm{x},m)

be a Lyapunov-type quantity which is parameterized by
m\in[0,1]_{N}\triangleq\{0,1/N,\dots,1\}
and is defined in (
35
). Define

m(\bm{x})=\max\Bigl\{m\in[0,1]_{N}:h(\bm{x},m)\leq\min_{k\in[K]}\bar{C}_{k}^{\star}([Nm])\Bigr\}.

(33)

and the
focus set
as the prefix
[Nm(x)]
.
Intuitively,
m(x)
is chosen as the largest fraction such that

h(\bm{x},m)
is covered by the minimum remaining budget across constraints.

The analysis then establishes three key properties:
(i)
majority conformity
—almost all arms in the focus set, except for
O(\sqrt{N})
arms, can follow the optimal single-armed policies
\bar{\pi}^{\star}_{i}
;
(ii)
almost non-shrinking
—the focus set shrinks by at most
O(\sqrt{N})
on average over time;
(iii)
sufficient coverage
—the complement size
1-m(\bm{X}_{t})
is controlled by
h(\bm{X}_{t},m(\bm{X}_{t}))
.

Projection-based Lyapunov function.

We firstly introduce some necessary single-armed quantities.
For each cost type
k\in[K]
, define

c^{\star}_{k,i}(s)\triangleq\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\bar{\pi}^{\star}_{i}(a\mid s)\,c_{k,i}(s,a)
and the vector

c^{\star}_{k}\triangleq(c^{\star}_{k,i})_{i\in[N]}
.
Similarly, define
r^{\star}_{i}(s)\triangleq\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\bar{\pi}^{\star}_{i}(a\mid s)\,r_{i}(s,a)
and

r^{\star}\triangleq(r^{\star}_{i})_{i\in[N]}
.
We collect these vectors as
\mathcal{G}\triangleq\{c^{\star}_{1},\dots,c^{\star}_{K},r^{\star}\}
.
With Assumption
4.1
, the Markov chain induced by
\bar{\pi}^{\star}_{i}
converges to a unique stationary distribution, denoted by
\mu_{i}^{\star}=(\mu_{i}^{\star}(s))_{s\in\mathcal{S}}
. It is straightforward to verify that
\mu_{i}^{\star}(s)=y_{i}^{\star}(s,0)+y_{i}^{\star}(s,1)
. We scale the subset Lyapunov function introduced in
(
Zhang et al. 2025
)
, for any system state
\bm{x}
and subset
D\subseteq[N]
,

h_{P,\tau}(\bm{x},D)\triangleq\max_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\sup_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in D}\left\langle(\bm{x}_{i}-\mu_{i}^{\star})P_{i}^{\ell}/\gamma^{\ell},g_{i}\right\rangle\right|,

where

\gamma=4^{-\frac{1}{2\tau}},\quad\tau=(3+\log_{2}S)\tau_{\mathrm{unif}},\quad g_{i}\in\mathcal{G}.

(34)

Here
\tau_{\mathrm{unif}}
is the uniform mixing time defined in Assumption
4.1
. Then for any
m\in[0,1]_{N}
,

h(\bm{x},m)\triangleq\max_{m^{\prime}\in[0,1]_{N}:m^{\prime}\leq m}h_{P,\tau}\bigl(\bm{x},[Nm^{\prime}]\bigr),

(35)

Finally the Lyapunov function is defined as

V(\bm{x})=h(\bm{x},m(\bm{x}))+L_{h}(1-m(\bm{x})),

(36)

where
m(\bm{x})
is defined in (
33
) and

L_{h}=2\max\{c_{\max},r_{\max}\}C_{\tau},\quad C_{\tau}=16\tau,\quad\tau=(3+\log_{2}S)\tau_{\mathrm{unif}}.

(37)

Moreover, by the
L_{h}
-Lipschitz property of
h(\cdot,m(\cdot))
in

Zhang et al. 2025
, we have for any system state
\bm{x}
,

V(\bm{x})=h(\bm{x},m(\bm{x}))+L_{h}(1-m(\bm{x}))\leq h(\bm{x},0)+L_{h}m(\bm{x})+L_{h}(1-m(\bm{x}))=L_{h}.

(38)

Bounding the drift of
V
yields control of the long-run average
V(\bm{X}_{t})
, and together with the focus-set lemmas
this leads to an
O(1/\sqrt{N})
optimality gap for the ID policy with reassignment.

C.3
Empirical ID policy

In this subsection, we introduce the ID policy for the empirical system. The empirical LP is obtained from the relaxation (
2
) by
replacing
P_{i}
with
\widehat{P}_{i}
in the flow-balance constraints. Let

\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}}
be its optimal value, and let

\bigl(\widehat{y}^{\star}_{i}(s,a)\bigr)_{i\in[N],\,s\in\mathcal{S},\,a\in\mathcal{A}}

be an optimal solution. The empirical optimal single-arm policies

\{\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
are defined from
\widehat{y}^{\star}_{i}
in the same way
that
\{\bar{\pi}^{\star}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
are defined from
y_{i}^{\star}
. Plugging

\widehat{y}^{\star}_{i}
and
\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}_{i}
into the ID-policy construction gives the
empirical ID policy
\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}
. For any policy
\pi
, we write

\widehat{\rho}^{\pi}\in\mathbb{R}^{S^{N}}
for its gain under the empirical

N
-armed system.

Appendix D
Proof details of the lemmas used in Theorem
4.1

D.1
Proofs of model accuracy lemmas

Proof D.1

Proof of Lemma
6.1

Fix
i\in[N]
, let
e_{sa}\in\mathbb{R}^{SA}
be the row-selector
vector whose
(s,a)
-th entry is
1
and others
0
. Let
P_{i},\widehat{P}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{SA\times S}
be matrices with
P_{i}(s,a,s^{\prime})=P(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)
and
\widehat{P}_{i}(s,a,s^{\prime})=\widehat{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a)
.
Then by definition,

\max_{s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\|P_{i}(\cdot\mid s,a)-\widehat{P}_{i}(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{1}=\max_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\max_{v\in\{-1,1\}^{\mathcal{S}}}e_{sa}^{\top}\left(P_{i}-\widehat{P}_{i}\right)v.

Now fixing some
s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A},v\in\{-1,1\}^{\mathcal{S}}
, we have by Hoeffding’s inequality that

\mathbb{P}\left(e_{sa}^{\top}\left(P_{i}-\widehat{P}_{i}\right)v\geq\sqrt{\frac{2\log(1/\eta^{\prime})}{n}}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2n}{4}\sqrt{\frac{2\log(1/\eta^{\prime})}{n}}^{2}\right)=\eta^{\prime}.

Setting
\eta^{\prime}=\frac{\eta}{NSA2^{S}}
and taking a union bound over all
s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A},v\in\{-1,1\}^{\mathcal{S}},i\in[N]
, we obtain the bound in the statement of the lemma.

Proof D.2

Proof of Lemma
6.2

By Lemma
D.3
, for any fixed
(\bm{s},\bm{a})\in\mathcal{S}^{N}\times\mathcal{A}^{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\bm{P}(\cdot\mid\bm{s},\bm{a})-\widehat{\bm{P}}(\cdot\mid\bm{s},\bm{a})\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1}^{\prime},\dots,s_{N}^{\prime}}\left|\prod_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}(s_{i}^{\prime}\mid s_{i},a_{i})-\prod_{i=1}^{N}\widehat{P}_{i}(s_{i}^{\prime}\mid s_{i},a_{i})\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1}^{\prime},\dots,s_{N}^{\prime}}\left|\sum_{j=1}^{N}\Big(\prod_{i<j}\widehat{P}_{i}(s_{i}^{\prime}\mid s_{i},a_{i})\Big)\Big(P_{j}(s_{j}^{\prime}\mid s_{j},a_{j})-\widehat{P}_{j}(s_{j}^{\prime}\mid s_{j},a_{j})\Big)\Big(\prod_{i>j}P_{i}(s_{i}^{\prime}\mid s_{i},a_{i})\Big)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1}^{\prime},\dots,s_{N}^{\prime}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\prod_{i<j}\widehat{P}_{i}(s_{i}^{\prime}\mid s_{i},a_{i})\left|P_{j}(s_{j}^{\prime}\mid s_{j},a_{j})-\widehat{P}_{j}(s_{j}^{\prime}\mid s_{j},a_{j})\right|\prod_{i>j}P_{i}(s_{i}^{\prime}\mid s_{i},a_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\underbrace{\left(\sum_{s_{1}^{\prime},\dots,s_{j-1}^{\prime}}\prod_{i<j}\widehat{P}_{i}(s_{i}^{\prime}\mid s_{i},a_{i})\right)}_{=1}\times\left(\sum_{s_{j}^{\prime}}\left|P_{j}(s_{j}^{\prime}\mid s_{j},a_{j})-\widehat{P}_{j}(s_{j}^{\prime}\mid s_{j},a_{j})\right|\right)\times\underbrace{\left(\sum_{s_{j+1}^{\prime},\dots,s_{N}^{\prime}}\prod_{i>j}P_{i}(s_{i}^{\prime}\mid s_{i},a_{i})\right)}_{=1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left\|P_{j}(\cdot\mid s_{j},a_{j})-\widehat{P}_{j}(\cdot\mid s_{j},a_{j})\right\|_{1}\leq N\delta.

It follows that

\max_{\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}^{N},\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}^{N}}\left\|\bm{P}(\cdot\mid\bm{s},\bm{a})-\widehat{\bm{P}}(\cdot\mid\bm{s},\bm{a})\right\|_{1}\leq N\delta

as desired. And accordingly,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{P}^{\pi}-\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\pi}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}=

\displaystyle\max_{\bm{s}}\left\|\bm{P}^{\pi}(\bm{s},\cdot)-\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\pi}(\bm{s},\cdot)\right\|_{1}\leq\max_{\bm{s}}\sum_{\bm{a}}\pi(\bm{a}\mid\bm{s})\left\|\bm{P}(\cdot\mid\bm{s},\bm{a})-\widehat{\bm{P}}(\cdot\mid\bm{s},\bm{a})\right\|_{1}\leq N\delta.

Finally, we record the following standard identity, which is used in the proof of Lemma
6.2
.

Lemma D.3

For any real numbers
a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
for
i=1,\dots,N
, the following identity holds:

\prod_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}-\prod_{i=1}^{N}b_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\prod_{i<j}b_{i}\right)(a_{j}-b_{j})\left(\prod_{i>j}a_{i}\right).

Proof D.4

Proof
We proceed by induction on
N
. The base case
N=2
follows from:

a_{1}a_{2}-b_{1}b_{2}=(a_{1}-b_{1})a_{2}+b_{1}(a_{2}-b_{2}).

Assume the identity holds for
N-1
terms. Then,

\prod_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}-\prod_{i=1}^{N}b_{i}=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}a_{i}\right)a_{N}-\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}b_{i}\right)b_{N}=\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}a_{i}-\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}b_{i}\right)a_{N}+\left(\prod_{i=1}^{N-1}b_{i}\right)(a_{N}-b_{N}).

Applying the inductive hypothesis to the first RHS term completes the proof.

D.2
Proof of Lemma
6.4

We prove Lemma
6.4
. The goal of Lemma
6.4
is to bound
\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}(\bm{s}_{0})
, which is the second term in the decomposition (
15
), so we need to work with the
empirical
system. The proof mirrors that of
Lemma
6.3
, but with the empirical LP solution
and the empirical kernel. We first verify that the empirical single-arm
chains needed to define the empirical Lyapunov function are well behaved.

D.2.1
Markov chain under empirical model

Lemma D.5

Under the conditions of Lemma
6.4
, for every
i\in[N]
, the Markov
chain induced by
\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}_{i}
under
\widehat{P}_{i}
is unichain and aperiodic,
with mixing time at most
\tau
, where
\tau
is defined in (
34
).

Proof D.6

Proof.
Fix
i\in[N]
. Let

Q_{i}(s,s^{\prime})=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}_{i}(a\mid s)P_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a),\widehat{Q}_{i}(s,s^{\prime})=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}_{i}(a\mid s)\widehat{P}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a).

Although
\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}_{i}
is computed from the empirical model, it is still a
stationary policy for the true single-arm MDP. Hence
Assumption
4.1
implies that
Q_{i}
is unichain,
aperiodic, and has mixing time at most
\tau_{\mathrm{unif}}
. Moreover, by convexity of
the
\ell_{1}
norm,

\left\lVert\widehat{Q}_{i}-Q_{i}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq\max_{s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}}\left\|\widehat{P}_{i}(\cdot\mid s,a)-P_{i}(\cdot\mid s,a)\right\|_{1}\leq\delta\leq\frac{1}{4S\tau_{\mathrm{unif}}(5+\log_{2}S)}.

Lemma
B.2
, applied with

\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{S}
,
Q=Q_{i}
, and

\widetilde{Q}=\widehat{Q}_{i}
, gives the desired conclusion.

Let
\widehat{\mu}^{\star}_{i}
denote the stationary distribution of the Markov chain
induced by
\widehat{\bar{\pi}}^{\star}_{i}
under
\widehat{P}_{i}
, whose existence and uniqueness
are guaranteed by Lemma
D.5
.

D.2.2
Lyapunov function for the empirical system

In this subsection we introduce the Lyapunov function for the empirical system. We firstly introduce the following subset Lyapunov function:

h_{\widehat{P},\tau}(\bm{x},D)=\max_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\sup_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i\in D}\left\langle(\bm{x}_{i}-\widehat{\mu}_{i}^{\star})\widehat{P}_{i}^{\ell}/\gamma^{\ell},g_{i}\right\rangle\right|,

where
\gamma,g_{i}\in\mathcal{G}
are the same as (
34
). Then we define

\widehat{h}(\bm{x},m)\triangleq\max_{m^{\prime}\in[0,1]_{N}:m^{\prime}\leq m}h_{\widehat{P},\tau}\bigl(\bm{x},[Nm^{\prime}]\bigr),

Finally, we define the Lyapunov function
\widehat{V}
for the empirical system as

\widehat{V}(\bm{x})=\widehat{h}(\bm{x},\widehat{m}(\bm{x}))+L_{h}(1-\widehat{m}(\bm{x})),

(39)

where
L_{h}
is the same as (
37
) and
\widehat{m}(\bm{x})
is defined as

\widehat{m}(\bm{x})=\max\Bigl\{m\in[0,1]_{N}:\widehat{h}(\bm{x},m)\leq\min_{k\in[K]}\widehat{\overline{C}}_{k}^{\star}([Nm])\Bigr\},

where

\widehat{\overline{C}}_{k}^{\star}(D)=\alpha_{k}N-\widehat{C}_{k}^{\star}(D)-\dfrac{\alpha_{k}}{3}\,|D|\cdot\mathbb{I}(k\not\in\mathcal{B}),

\widehat{C}_{k}^{\star}(D)\triangleq\sum_{i\in D}\widehat{C}_{k,i}^{\star}

and

\widehat{C}_{k,i}^{\star}\triangleq\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\widehat{y}_{i}^{\star}(s,a)\,c_{k,i}(s,a).

The focus set of
\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}
is
[N\widehat{m}(\bm{x})]
.

D.2.3
Completing the proof of Lemma
6.4

Proof D.7

Proof
The next two lemmas implement the two key parts for the Lyapunov framework: Lemma
D.8
provides the drift bound (Condition
(C1)
) and Lemma
D.9
provides the gap-dominance bound (Condition
(C2)
), both for the
empirical
quantities and the policy
\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}
.
Their proofs are identical to those for Lemmas
6.5
and
6.6
after replacing the true model by the empirical model; we omit the details.

Lemma D.8

Under the assumptions of Lemma
6.4
, the Lyapunov function
\widehat{V}(\cdot)
defined in (
39
) satisfies

\widehat{\mathbb{E}}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}\!\left[\widehat{V}(\bm{X}_{t+1})-\widehat{V}(\bm{X}_{t})\mid\bm{X}_{t}=\bm{x}\right]\leq-\rho_{V}\,\widehat{V}(\bm{x})+K_{V}/\sqrt{N},

(40)

where the constants
\rho_{V}
and
K_{V}
are the same as in (
22
).

Remark.

The constants in (
40
) are the same as those in the true system drift bound (Lemma
6.5
), because their definitions do not depend on the transition kernel, which is the only difference between the true and empirical systems.

Lemma D.9

Suppose the assumptions of Lemma
6.4
hold. Fix a system state
\bm{s}
, and let
A_{i}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}
be the action applied to arm
i
under
\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}
. Define

r^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}(\bm{s})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\!\left[r_{i}(s_{i},A_{i}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}})\right].

Then

\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}}-r^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}(\bm{s})\leq\frac{2r_{\max}+L_{h}}{L_{h}}\,\widehat{V}(\bm{x})+\frac{2r_{\max}K_{\mathrm{conf}}}{\sqrt{N}}.

Back to the proof of Lemma
6.4
.

We apply Lemma
6.7
with
Q=\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}
,
P=\bm{P}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}
,
R=\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}}
,
F=\widehat{V}
, and
\bar{r}=r^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}
. Lemmas
D.8
and
D.9
give the same constants as in Lemma
6.3
:

a=\rho_{V},b=K_{V}/\sqrt{N},c=\frac{2r_{\max}+L_{h}}{L_{h}},d=\frac{2r_{\max}K_{\mathrm{conf}}}{\sqrt{N}}.

Therefore,

\widehat{\rho}^{\mathrm{rel}}-\rho^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}(\bm{s}_{0})\leq\frac{2r_{\max}+L_{h}}{L_{h}\rho_{V}}\sup_{\bm{x}}|(\bm{P}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}-\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}})\widehat{V}(\bm{x})|+\left[\frac{(2r_{\max}+L_{h})K_{V}}{L_{h}\rho_{V}}+2r_{\max}K_{\mathrm{conf}}\right]\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}.

By Lemma
6.2
,
\left\lVert\bm{P}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}-\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq N\delta
. The empirical Lyapunov function satisfies
\|\widehat{V}\|_{\infty}\leq L_{h}
by the same Lipschitz argument used for
V
. Thus

\sup_{\bm{x}}|(\bm{P}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}}-\widehat{\bm{P}}^{\widehat{\pi}_{\mathrm{ID}}})\widehat{V}(\bm{x})|\leq L_{h}N\delta,

which gives the desired bound.

Appendix E
Proof of lemmas used in Theorem
4.3

E.1
Proof of Lemma
8.3

Proof E.1

Proof of Lemma
8.3
.
It suffices to bound
\left\lVert\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}
.
We calculate

\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}=U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}+(\widehat{U}-U)-\mathbf{1}(\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{\star})=U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}+(\widehat{U}-U)-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U},

using Lemma
8.1
in the second equality.
Then by triangle inequality we have that

\left\lVert\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\leq\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}+\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}+\left\lVert\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}.

Now subtracting both sides of the above inequality from
1
, we have

\displaystyle 1-\left\lVert\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}-\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}-\left\lVert\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}-\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}-\left\lVert\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{\geq}}1-\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}-\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu^{\star}_{\min}}}\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}

where in the final inequality
(*)
we used that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\left\lVert(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu^{\star}_{\min}}}\frac{1}{1-\left\lVert(\widehat{U}-U)\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}},

noting that
\left\lVert\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu^{\star}_{\min}}}\left\lVert\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu^{\star}_{\min}}}
.

To ensure
1-\left\lVert\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\geq\frac{1-\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}}{2}
, it is sufficient to guarantee that

\displaystyle 1-\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}-\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu^{\star}_{\min}}}\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1-\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}}{2}.

Simplifying the expression, this is equivalent to

\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\left(1+\frac{\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}}{\sqrt{\mu^{\star}_{\min}}}\right)\leq\frac{1-\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}}{2},

which is in turn equivalent to (
25
).

We note that (
25
) implies the previously used condition that
\left\lVert(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}<1
, since
\left\lVert(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}
and (
25
) implies that

\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}\leq 1/2<1.
Hence,
(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})^{-1}
is invertible.

E.2
Algebraic properties of
U,\widehat{U},\mu^{\star},\widehat{\mu},H_{U}
and
H_{\widehat{U}}

The following two lemmas that check various algebraic properties of
U,\widehat{U},\mu^{\star},\widehat{\mu},H_{U}
, and
H_{\widehat{U}}
, which will be used in the sequel.

Lemma E.2

We have
U\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
and
\widehat{U}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
. Moreover, if

I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star})
is invertible, then
H_{U}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{0}
.

Proof E.3

Proof.
We firstly prove
U\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
; the proof of
\widehat{U}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
is identical.
Since
P^{\pi^{\star}}
is row-stochastic and

\xi\mathbf{1}=(P_{\tilde{s}1}-P_{\tilde{s}0})\mathbf{1}=1-1=0,

we have

U\mathbf{1}=\bigl(P^{\pi^{\star}}-(c^{\pi^{\star}}-\alpha\mathbf{1})\xi\bigr)\mathbf{1}=P^{\pi^{\star}}\mathbf{1}-(c^{\pi^{\star}}-\alpha\mathbf{1})(\xi\mathbf{1})=\mathbf{1}.

Let
x=H_{U}\mathbf{1}=(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\mathbf{1}=(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{1}
(using that
\mu^{\star}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
). Then left-multiplying by
(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))
, we have

\displaystyle(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))x

\displaystyle=(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}\mathbf{1}-(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{1}+U\mathbf{1}-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{0}

using that
\mu^{\star}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
and that
U\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
in the final equality. But since
(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))
is invertible, the equality
(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))x=\mathbf{0}
implies that
x=\mathbf{0}
.

Lemma E.4

Suppose that
\left\lVert U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}<1
and
(
25
) holds. Then

\widehat{\mu}\widehat{U}=\widehat{\mu},\widehat{\mu}\mathbf{1}=1,
and

H_{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{0},\widehat{\mu}H_{\widehat{U}}=\mathbf{0},(I-\widehat{U})H_{\widehat{U}}=I-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}.

Moreover, there exists a row vector
x
such that

H_{\widehat{U}}-H_{U}=H_{U}(\widehat{U}-U)H_{\widehat{U}}+\mathbf{1}x.

Proof E.5

Proof.
Abbreviate
X=(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})^{-1}
. By the definition (
24
) of
\widehat{\mu}
, to show
\widehat{\mu}\widehat{U}=\widehat{\mu}
it suffices to show that

\mu^{\star}X\widehat{U}=\mu^{\star}X.

From the definition of
X
we have that

\displaystyle X(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})=I\implies

\displaystyle X+XUH_{U}-I=X\widehat{U}H_{U}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle(X+XUH_{U}-I)(I-U)=X\widehat{U}H_{U}(I-U)=X\widehat{U}(I-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star})

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle X\widehat{U}=(X+XUH_{U}-I)(I-U)+X\widehat{U}\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}

\displaystyle\quad\;\;\,=X-XU+XUH_{U}-XUH_{U}U-I+U+X\widehat{U}\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star},

where we used the property that
H_{U}(I-U)=I-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}
.
Now left-multiplying by
\mu^{\star}
, and also using the facts that
\widehat{U}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
and
\widehat{U}\mathbf{1}=U\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
from Lemma
E.2
, we have that

\displaystyle\mu^{\star}X\widehat{U}

\displaystyle=\mu^{\star}\left(X-XU+XUH_{U}-XUH_{U}U-I+U+X\widehat{U}\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right)

\displaystyle=\mu^{\star}X+\mu^{\star}XU(-I+H_{U}-H_{U}U+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star})=\mu^{\star}X,

where in the final equality we used that
-I+H_{U}-H_{U}U+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}=0
, which is equivalent to the fact that
H_{U}(I-U)=I-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}
.
Hence we have that
\widehat{\mu}\widehat{U}=\widehat{\mu}
as desired.

Now we check that
\widehat{\mu}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
. The condition
\left\lVert(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}<1
(which is implied by (
25
) as mentioned above) implies the Neumann series expansion
(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})^{-1}=\sum_{t\geq 0}((\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})^{t}
. Hence by the definition (
24
) of
\widehat{\mu}
we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}\mathbf{1}=\mu^{\star}(I-(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})^{-1}\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 0}\mu^{\star}((\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})^{t}\mathbf{1}=\mu^{\star}\mathbf{1}+\sum_{t\geq 0}\mu^{\star}((\widehat{U}-U)H_{U})^{t}(\widehat{U}-U)H_{U}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1},

where in the final equality we used that
\mu^{\star}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
and that
H_{U}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{0}
(from Lemma
E.2
).

Having checked
\widehat{\mu}\mathbf{1}=1
, we can use an analogous proof as for Lemma
E.2
to show
H_{\widehat{U}}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{0}
.

Next we check that
\widehat{\mu}H_{\widehat{U}}=\mathbf{0}
. From the Neumann series for
(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}
(since
\left\lVert\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}\right\rVert_{\mu^{\star}\to\mu^{\star}}<1
by Lemma
8.3
) we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mu}H_{\widehat{U}}=\widehat{\mu}(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-\widehat{\mu}\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 0}\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu})^{t}-\widehat{\mu}=\widehat{\mu}I+\sum_{t\geq 0}\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu})(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu})^{t}-\widehat{\mu}=\mathbf{0},

where we used that
\widehat{\mu}(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu})=\mathbf{0}
, which follows from combining the facts
\widehat{\mu}\widehat{U}=\widehat{\mu}
and
\widehat{\mu}\mathbf{1}=1
.

Finally we show that
(I-\widehat{U})H_{\widehat{U}}=I-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}
. We have

\displaystyle(I-\widehat{U})H_{\widehat{U}}=(I-\widehat{U}+\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu})H_{\widehat{U}}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}H_{\widehat{U}}

\displaystyle=(I-\widehat{U}+\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu})\left((I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}\right)-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}H_{\widehat{U}}

\displaystyle=I-(I-\widehat{U}+\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu})\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}H_{\widehat{U}}=I-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}+\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu},

where the final equality used
\widehat{U}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
,
\widehat{\mu}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
and
\widehat{\mu}H_{\widehat{U}}=\mathbf{0}
.

Note the resolvent identity
A^{-1}-B^{-1}=A^{-1}(B-A)B^{-1}=B^{-1}(B-A)A^{-1}
and the fact that
I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu})
and
I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star})
are invertible due to the assumptions and Lemma
8.3
. We have

\displaystyle(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}

\displaystyle=(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}\left((I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))-(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}

\displaystyle=(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}\left(\widehat{U}-U-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}

\displaystyle=(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-\mathbf{1}(\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{\star})(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}

(41)

using that
(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
in the final equality, which follows from Lemma
E.2
.
Next we have

(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}\\
=H_{U}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}

(42)

and

\displaystyle H_{U}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}

\displaystyle=H_{U}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)H_{\widehat{U}}+H_{U}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}

\displaystyle=H_{U}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)H_{\widehat{U}}+H_{U}\mathbf{0}=H_{U}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)H_{\widehat{U}},

(43)

where we used
U\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
and
\widehat{U}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
from Lemma
E.2
.
Combining these calculations, we have that

\displaystyle H_{\widehat{U}}-H_{U}=

\displaystyle(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}-(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))^{-1}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-\mathbf{1}(\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{\star})(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H_{U}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}

\displaystyle-\mathbf{1}(\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{\star})(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(iii)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle H_{U}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)H_{\widehat{U}}+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-\mathbf{1}(\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{\star})(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}

where in steps
(i),(ii),
and
(iii)
we used equations (
41
), (
42
), and (
43
), respectively. Hence the desired equality is true for

x=\mu^{\star}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-(\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{\star})(I-(\widehat{U}-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}))^{-1}-\widehat{\mu}+\mu^{\star}.

E.3
Proof of Lemma
8.4

Before checking the feasibility of
\widehat{y}
, we first show some useful facts about
W
. Firstly we write the entries of
W
more explicitly. For
s\neq\tilde{s}
,

\displaystyle W_{s,s0}=\pi^{\star}(0\mid s),\ W_{s,s1}=\pi^{\star}(1\mid s),\ W_{s,\tilde{s}0}=(1-\alpha)-\pi^{\star}(0\mid s),\ W_{s,\tilde{s}1}=\alpha-\pi^{\star}(1\mid s),

(44)

and all other entries of the row
W_{s,\cdot}
are zero. For

s=\tilde{s}
,

\displaystyle W_{\tilde{s},\tilde{s}0}=1-\alpha,\quad W_{\tilde{s},\tilde{s}1}=\alpha,

(45)

and all other entries of the row
W_{\tilde{s},\cdot}
are zero. Then we have the following lemma.

Lemma E.6

W
satisfies the following properties:

WJ=I
;

W\tilde{e}=\alpha\mathbf{1}
;

W\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
;

WP=U
;

W\widehat{P}=\widehat{U}
.

Proof E.7

Proof
1. (Proof of
WJ=I
.) Let
\mu
be an arbitrary row vector of size
S
and let
y=\mu W
. For any non-neutral state
s\neq\tilde{s}
, we have

(yJ)(s)=\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}y(s,a)=\mu(s)\sum_{a}\pi^{\star}(a\mid s)=\mu(s).

For the neutral state
\tilde{s}
, using
\pi^{\star}(0\mid s)+\pi^{\star}(1\mid s)=1
for any
s\in\mathcal{S}
, we have

\displaystyle(yJ)(\tilde{s})=y(\tilde{s},0)+y(\tilde{s},1)

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)(\mu\mathbf{1})+\alpha(\mu\mathbf{1})-\sum_{s\neq\tilde{s}}\big(\pi^{\star}(0\mid s)+\pi^{\star}(1\mid s)\big)\mu(s)

\displaystyle=(\mu\mathbf{1})-\sum_{s\neq\tilde{s}}\mu(s)=\mu(\tilde{s}).

Hence
yJ=\mu
holds for every
\mu
, that is
\mu(WJ)=\mu
for all
\mu
,
so
WJ=I
.

2. (Proof of
W\tilde{e}=\alpha\mathbf{1}
.)
Recall
\tilde{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{SA}
is defined by
\tilde{e}(s,a)=\mathbb{I}(a=1)
.
Again let
\mu
be an arbitrary row vector of size
S
and let
y=\mu W
. Then

\displaystyle y\tilde{e}=\sum_{s\in S}y(s,1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\neq\tilde{s}}\mu(s)\pi^{\star}(1\mid s)+y(\tilde{s},1)=\sum_{s\neq\tilde{s}}\mu(s)\pi^{\star}(1\mid s)+\alpha(\mu\mathbf{1})-\sum_{s\neq\tilde{s}}\mu(s)\pi^{\star}(1\mid s)=\alpha(\mu\mathbf{1}).

Equivalently,
\mu(W\tilde{e})=\alpha(\mu\mathbf{1})=\mu(\alpha\mathbf{1})
for all
\mu
, so
W\tilde{e}=\alpha\mathbf{1}
.

3. (Proof of
W\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
.)
Let
\mathbf{1}_{S}
denote the all-ones vector in
\mathbb{R}^{S}
and

\mathbf{1}_{SA}
the all-ones vector in
\mathbb{R}^{SA}
.
By definition of
J
, we have
J\mathbf{1}_{S}=\mathbf{1}_{SA}
.
Therefore, using
WJ=I
from the previous part, we have

W\mathbf{1}_{SA}=WJ\mathbf{1}_{S}=I\mathbf{1}_{S}=\mathbf{1}_{S}.

4. (Proof of
W\widehat{P}=\widehat{U}
.)
Take an arbitrary
\mu
and set
y=\mu W
.
Then

y\widehat{P}=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}y(s,a)\widehat{P}_{sa}.

For
s\neq\tilde{s}
, we have
y(s,a)=\mu(s)\pi^{\star}(a\mid s)
, so

\sum_{a\in\{0,1\}}y(s,a)\widehat{P}_{sa}=\mu(s)e_{s}^{\top}\widehat{P}^{\pi^{\star}}.

For the neutral state,

\displaystyle y(\tilde{s},0)\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}0}+y(\tilde{s},1)\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}1}

\displaystyle=\Big((1-\alpha)(\mu\mathbf{1})-\sum_{s\neq\tilde{s}}\pi^{\star}(0\mid s)\mu(s)\Big)\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}0}+\Big(\alpha(\mu\mathbf{1})-\sum_{s\neq\tilde{s}}\pi^{\star}(1\mid s)\mu(s)\Big)\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}1}

\displaystyle=\mu(\tilde{s})e_{s}^{\top}\widehat{P}^{\pi^{\star}}+\left(\alpha(\mu\mathbf{1})-\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}c^{\pi^{\star}}(s)\mu(s)\right)\widehat{\xi}

since
\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}1}=\widehat{P}_{\tilde{s}0}+\widehat{\xi}
and
\pi^{\star}(0\mid s)=1-c^{\pi^{\star}}(s)
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
. Summing over all states we have

\displaystyle y\widehat{P}

\displaystyle=\left(\alpha(\mu\mathbf{1})-\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}c^{\pi^{\star}}(s)\mu(s)\right)\widehat{\xi}+\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)e_{s}^{\top}\widehat{P}^{\pi^{\star}}=\mu\left(\left(\alpha\mathbf{1}-c^{\pi^{\star}}\right)\widehat{\xi}+\widehat{P}^{\pi^{\star}}\right)=\mu\widehat{U}.

We have thus shown that
\mu W\widehat{P}=y\widehat{P}=\mu\widehat{U}
, so we have
W\widehat{P}=\widehat{U}
.

5. (Proof of
WP=U
.)
The proof of this fact is analogous to the previous part after replacing
\widehat{P},\widehat{U},\widehat{\xi}
by
P,U,\xi
, respectively.

We now prove Lemma
8.4
.

Proof E.8

Proof of Lemma
8.4
.
First we check the bound (
26
). Since
\widehat{y}=\widehat{\mu}W
and
y^{\star}=\mu^{\star}W
, using Lemma
8.1
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{y}-y^{\star}=(\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{\star})W=\widehat{\mu}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)H_{U}W

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{i}-y_{i}^{\star}=(\widehat{\mu}-\mu^{\star})We_{i}=\widehat{\mu}\left(\widehat{U}-U\right)H_{U}We_{i}

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{y}_{i}-y_{i}^{\star}\right|\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mu}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\|We_{i}\right\|_{\infty}.

(46)

We have
\left\|We_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\max\{\alpha,1-\alpha\}\leq 1
for
i=(\tilde{s},0),(\tilde{s},1)
, and
\left\|We_{i}\right\|_{\infty}=1
for the remaining
i=(s,a)
which have
\pi^{\star}(a\mid s)=1
. Also
We_{i}=\mathbf{0}
if
i=(s,a)
where
s\neq\tilde{s}
and
\pi^{\star}(a\mid s)=0
. In all cases we have
\left\|We_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Combining the above bound with the fact that
\left\lVert\widehat{\mu}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}=1
, we obtain the desired bound.

We now verify feasibility of
\widehat{y}
for the perturbed primal
LP (
11
). Using Lemma
E.6
and Lemma
E.4
, we have

\widehat{y}\tilde{e}=\widehat{\mu}W\tilde{e}=\widehat{\mu}(\alpha\mathbf{1})=\alpha,\widehat{y}\mathbf{1}=\widehat{\mu}W\mathbf{1}=\widehat{\mu}\mathbf{1}=1,\widehat{y}\widehat{P}=\widehat{\mu}W\widehat{P}=\widehat{\mu}\widehat{U}=\widehat{\mu}=\widehat{\mu}WJ=\widehat{y}J..

Thus it remains to check nonnegativity. If
i=(s,a)
,
s\neq\tilde{s}
, and

\pi^{\star}(a\mid s)=0
, then
y^{\star}_{i}=0
and
We_{i}=\mathbf{0}
, so
(
46
) gives
\widehat{y}_{i}=0
. For indices

i
with
y^{\star}_{i}>0
, using
\left\lVert We_{i}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\leq 1
and
(
46
),

\displaystyle|\widehat{y}_{i}-y^{\star}_{i}|

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\widehat{\mu}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\|We_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\lVert\widehat{U}-U\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\left\lVert H_{U}\right\rVert_{\infty\to\infty}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{2}\min_{j:y^{\star}_{j}>0}y^{\star}_{j}\leq\frac{1}{2}y^{\star}_{i}

where
(*)
follows from the assumption (
27
). Hence

\widehat{y}_{i}\geq y^{\star}_{i}/2>0
for all
i
with
y^{\star}_{i}>0
.
Therefore
\widehat{y}\geq 0
, and all constraints of
(
11
) are satisfied.

E.4
Proof of Lemma
8.5

Proof E.9

Proof.
Each row of
W
is only supported on indices
i
such that
y^{\star}_{i}>0
, and hence where (
28
) holds. Therefore we have

W(r+\lambda^{\star}\tilde{e}+Ph^{\star})=W(\zeta^{\star}\mathbf{1}+Jh^{\star}).

Using the properties of
W
from Lemma
E.6
that
WP=U,W\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1},WJ=I
, and
W\tilde{e}=\alpha\mathbf{1}
, the above can be simplified to

Wr+\lambda^{\star}\alpha\mathbf{1}+Uh^{\star}=\zeta^{\star}\mathbf{1}+h^{\star}.

Eliminating
\zeta^{\star}
using that
\zeta^{\star}=\mu^{\star}Wr+\lambda^{\star}\alpha
(since the primal value
y^{\star}r=\mu^{\star}Wr
must be equal to the dual value
\xi^{\star}-\alpha\lambda^{\star}
), we obtain

Wr+\lambda^{\star}\alpha\mathbf{1}+Uh^{\star}=(\mu^{\star}Wr+\lambda^{\star}\alpha)\mathbf{1}+h^{\star}

which simplifies further to

(I-U)h^{\star}=Wr-\mu^{\star}Wr\mathbf{1}.

The solutions to this Poisson-like equation can be characterized using the assumed invertibility of
(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}))
. Rewriting the equation as

\left(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star})\right)h^{\star}=(I-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star})Wr+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}h,

we obtain

\displaystyle h^{\star}

\displaystyle=\left(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star})\right)^{-1}\left((I-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star})Wr+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}h^{\star}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star})\right)^{-1}Wr-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}Wr+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}h^{\star}=H_{U}Wr+\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star}h^{\star}

using that
\left(I-(U-\mathbf{1}\mu^{\star})\right)^{-1}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{1}
, which follows from the fact that
H_{U}\mathbf{1}=\mathbf{0}
(Lemma
E.2
).

E.5
Proof of Lemma
8.6
and Lemma
8.7

Before checking the feasibility of
(\widehat{\zeta},\widehat{\lambda},\widehat{h})
, we compute one more useful fact about
Wr
.

Lemma E.10

We have

Wr=r^{\pi^{\star}}+(c^{\pi^{\star}}-\alpha\mathbf{1})(r(\tilde{s},0)-r(\tilde{s},1)).

Proof E.11

Proof.
For
s\neq\tilde{s}
, using (
44
) we have

\displaystyle(Wr)(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime}a^{\prime}}W_{s,s^{\prime}a^{\prime}}r(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\pi^{\star}(0\mid s)r(s,0)+\pi^{\star}(1\mid s)r(s,1)+\left((1-\alpha)-\pi^{\star}(0\mid s)\right)r(\tilde{s},0)+\left(\alpha-\pi^{\star}(1\mid s)\right)r(\tilde{s},1)

\displaystyle=r^{\pi^{\star}}(s)-\alpha r(\tilde{s},0)+\alpha r(\tilde{s},1)+c^{\pi^{\star}}(s)r(\tilde{s},0)-c^{\pi^{\star}}(s)r(\tilde{s},1)

\displaystyle=r^{\pi^{\star}}(s)+(c^{\pi^{\star}}(s)-\alpha)(r(\tilde{s},0)-r(\tilde{s},1)).

For
s=\tilde{s}
, using (
45
) we have

\displaystyle(Wr)(\tilde{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{s^{\prime}a^{\prime}}W_{\tilde{s},s^{\prime}a^{\prime}}r(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})=(1-\alpha)r(\tilde{s},0)+\alpha r(\tilde{s},1)

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)r(\tilde{s},0)+\alpha r(\tilde{s},1)+r^{\pi^{\star}}(\tilde{s})-\pi^{\star}(1\mid\tilde{s})r(\tilde{s},1)-\pi^{\star}(0\mid\tilde{s})r(\tilde{s},0)

\displaystyle=r^{\pi^{\star}}(\tilde{s})+(c^{\pi^{\star}}(\tilde{s})-\alpha)(r(\tilde{s},0)-r(\tilde{s},1)).

Therefore we have that

Wr=r^{\pi^{\star}}+(c^{\pi^{\star}}-\alpha\mathbf{1})(r(\tilde{s},0)-r(\tilde{s},1))

as desired.

Proof E.12

Proof of Lemma
8.6
.
From the fact that
(I-\widehat{U})H_{\widehat{U}}=I-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}
from Lemma
E.4
, we have that

\widehat{h}-\widehat{U}\widehat{h}=Wr-\mathbf{1}\widehat{\mu}Wr

which implies, after rearranging and using
\widehat{\mu}Wr=\widehat{\zeta}-\widehat{\lambda}\alpha
from the definition (
30
) of
\widehat{\zeta}
, that

\displaystyle\widehat{h}+\widehat{\zeta}\mathbf{1}=Wr+\widehat{U}\widehat{h}+\widehat{\lambda}\alpha\mathbf{1}=Wr+\widehat{P}^{\pi^{\star}}\widehat{h}-\left(c^{\pi^{\star}}-\alpha\mathbf{1}\right)\widehat{\xi}\widehat{h}+\widehat{\lambda}\alpha\mathbf{1}.

(47)

Using Lemma
E.10
as well as the definition (
29
) of
\widehat{\lambda}=r(\tilde{s},0)-r(\tilde{s},1)-\widehat{\xi}\widehat{h}
, we can compute that
